<b>Is an actuator at its limit before the falls?</b> By the rule the knee torque is (13 % of the samples of the last second at or above 95 % of the limit, against 1 % in the controls), but it reaches the limit only from 0.3 s before the fall, after pitch and height have left the controls: it is the robot going down, not what starts it. MPM's knee is at that limit in 8 % of the samples all the time without falling. The joint speed limits are not enforced in these runs, as far as the records show: the knee exceeds its configured limit in ordinary steps of both policies (95th percentile 1.04 times the limit for RFT, 1.31 for MPM).

<b>Does MPM keep more margin at +30°?</b> No. At the same places on the slope MPM uses more of the knee torque (95th percentile 0.98 of the limit against 0.64 for RFT trials that reach the goal), more ankle torque, and leans as far or further (95th percentile of the pelvis pitch 14.3° against 11.9°); the lowest forward speed is the same. MPM walks closer to the knee's torque limit than RFT does, not further from it.

<b>Is there a trigger both meet and only MPM recovers from?</b> None by the rule. At +30° the loaded sole moves downhill by more than 5 cm in 0.1 s at the start of almost every stance of both policies (4.8 such slips per metre for RFT, 5.0 for MPM), so every fall and every control window has one and it singles out nothing; no step is missed by the 1.5-times rule in any trial. The "slip" of the rule is therefore every touchdown at this slope and not an event, as the seventh check points out, and the change of knee torque in the 0.3 s after it, which an earlier version of this text read as a reaction, is the release of the torque the knee carries at touchdown (about 115 N m for MPM against 53 N m for RFT). What differs between the policies is that torque at touchdown (Figure W1). The pelvis pitch after touchdown gives no consistent difference (its sign reverses within the stance), so nothing is claimed about it.

<b>Reading, exploratory.</b> RFT's falls at +30° are forward pitch-overs that take less than a second, without a warning in any measured quantity before that, without a discrete trigger and without an actuator limit ahead of them. MPM does not walk with more margin: it leans as far and works at the knee's torque limit. What MPM does differently in every stance is push with the knee at touchdown (about 115 N m against 53 N m). Whether that is why MPM does not pitch over is a hypothesis this data cannot test, because MPM never falls here and RFT's successful and failing trials step alike. A test would need new runs, for example the MPM policy with the knee torque limited to what RFT uses, or RFT with a push at the pelvis of known size at +25°.

<b>Causal test: does the MPM policy need its knee torque to climb?</b> Checked on 2026-11-25 (eighth check: every number reproduced from the raw files, the rule preceded the runs; passed with corrections to the wording, applied here). Asked by the lead for Junnosuke after the follow-up above; the rule was written and committed before any run (task record, definitions block, third entry of 2026-11-25). Only the torque limit of the two knee joints is lowered; the policy (MPM, final checkpoint) and everything else are unchanged. The limit is set before the simulation is built; every run records it as read back from the simulation, and the solver's joint force range holds the cap for the two knees and the configured values for all other joints. That read-back and the way the goals follow the limit are the evidence that the cap acts; that the largest recorded knee torque equals the cap adds nothing, because the recorded torque is clipped with the same limit. Beach sand, 0.50 m bed, 1.0 m/s, the default bed setting of the slope sweep, 40 trials per step (seeds 41 to 48, 5 starts each, the starts of the merged runs); OSMO, L40. Control with the new code and no cap: 10 of 10 at +30°, as in the merged runs. 

<b>Result at +30°: necessary, by the rule fixed beforehand.</b> Goals of the MPM policy with the knee torque limited to 139 (as configured), 118, 104, 89 and 70 N m: 40 of 40, 28 of 40, 13 of 40, 2 of 40, 0 of 40. The goals fall step by step with the limit, and every failure but one is a fall forward, the way RFT fails. At 89 N m MPM reaches 2 of 40 goals against 40 of 40 uncapped (Fisher's exact test, p = 2e-20), and the cap binds (a knee at the limit in 33 % of the samples on the slope). The 89 N m step is at the upper end of what RFT uses at +30°, not below it: the 95th percentile of RFT's knee torque is 0.64 of the limit in the windows matched to its falls (seventh check) and 0.49 to 0.59 of it (68 to 82 N m; both knees pooled, and the larger knee per sample) over all samples on the slope of its trials that reach the goal. RFT with the full limit reaches 13 of 40 there: more than the MPM policy capped to 89 N m (2 of 40; Fisher's exact test, p = 0.003). RFT's gait gets further on less knee torque. The velocity error of the trials that still reach the goal rises with the cap (0.13, 0.16, 0.19, 0.23 m/s; the last value is two trials).

<b>At 89 N m the MPM policy still climbs +20° and +25°.</b> It reaches 40 of 40 at +25° although the cap binds there (17 % of the samples at the limit; verdict by the rule for this step: not necessary), and 40 of 40 at +20°, where the cap hardly binds (6 %). That does not make the knee torque unimportant below +30°: 89 N m is RFT's use at +30° only. At +25° RFT's 95th percentile is 36 to 39 N m and at +20° 32 to 34 N m, and with 70 N m, still about twice what RFT uses at +25°, the MPM policy reaches 24 of 40 there while RFT reaches 39 of 40. So the MPM policy's gait needs more knee torque than RFT's on +25° as well, where both climb. <b>With the product page's knee torque.</b> Unitree lists two different knee numbers: its product page gives a maximum knee torque of 90 N m (G1) and 120 N m (G1 EDU), as cited in the seventh check; its description files (URDF, MJCF) give 139 N m, which is what the simulation uses. The same files give 35 N m for the ankle joints, where the simulation uses 50 N m. Which numbers hold for the robot of the hardware test is not settled here. With the knee at 89 N m the MPM policy reaches 40 of 40, 40 of 40 and 2 of 40 at +20°, +25° and +30°; with 118 N m 40 of 40, 40 of 40 and 28 of 40. These cells change the torque limit only: the simulated knee has no torque against speed curve and no speed limit, and the uncapped MPM policy holds the knee at its limit while it moves at about 12 rad/s at +30° (median over the samples at 95 % of the limit or above; configured speed limit 20 rad/s, not enforced).

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| +30° | 104 | 0.75 | 13 / 40 | 27 fall forward | 23 | yes | 104 | 7.1 ± 0.9 (40) |
| +30° | 89 (near the product page's 90 N m, G1; upper end of RFT's use at this slope) | 0.64 | 2 / 40 | 38 fall forward | 33 | yes | 89 | 7.9 ± 1.1 (40) |

<b>Sand under the sole.</b> "Less sand moves under MPM's sole than under RFT's" was the one checked difference between the two. It changes with the knee torque limit: per stance at +30° the sand under the MPM policy's sole moves 5.1 cm at 139 N m, 6.1 cm at 118 N m, 7.1 cm at 104 N m, 7.9 cm at 89 N m, 11.0 cm at 70 N m, against 6.4 cm under RFT; at +25° 3.3 cm at 139 N m, 3.4 cm at 118 N m, 4.1 cm at 89 N m, 6.1 cm at 70 N m, against 4.5 cm under RFT; at +20° 2.2 cm at 139 N m, 2.2 cm at 118 N m, 2.4 cm at 89 N m, against 3.5 cm under RFT. Failed trials are included up to the fall; at +30° about half of the rise comes from the trials that fail (in the trials that reach the goal the checker finds 5.1, 5.6, 6.0 and 6.8 cm at 139, 118, 104 and 89 N m, the last on two trials). With the knee capped to 89 N m more sand moves under the MPM policy's sole than under RFT's at +30° only; at +25° and +20° it is still less, and at +25° it passes RFT's value only at 70 N m. So: less knee torque, more sand motion under the MPM policy's sole, at +30° and +25°. Whether the smaller sand motion under MPM comes from its knee torque is not tested, and the sand motion may be a sign of the more crouched gait under the cap and not a mechanism of its own.

<b>What this shows and what it does not.</b> Shown: the MPM policy as trained needs knee torque above 89 N m to climb +30°, and above 70 N m to climb +25° reliably; with less it pitches over forward, the way RFT fails. The outcome of the MPM policy depends on its knee torque. The difference between the two policies' outcomes was not tested, and the knee push is not shown to be why MPM succeeds where RFT fails: capped to 89 N m the MPM policy reaches 2 of 40, fewer than RFT with the full limit (13 of 40), and the cap was put on the MPM policy only, so it cannot say what RFT lacks. RFT has the same limits and uses less of the knee. The knee torque is what the MPM policy's own gait requires, not a quantity that by itself decides which policy climbs. Also not shown: whether the dependence is special to the knee (a cap on the hip pitch joints is run as a control), that no policy could climb +30° with less torque (one trained with the lower limit might find another way; none was trained), why training on MPM sand led to this use of the knee and training on RFT did not, and anything about level sand, where MPM's knee torque stays far below the limit. One checkpoint, beach sand, the default bed setting.

<b>The three policies with the knee limited to about the product page's values.</b> Checked on 2026-11-25 (ninth check, corrections applied); reading committed before the runs. RFT and MJWarp were run with the same knee limits as MPM, 40 trials per cell with the same seeds, starts and bed setting; only the knee torque limit differs from the merged runs (ankles 50 N m, no torque against speed curve). Goals of 40 at +30° (MJWarp / RFT / MPM): 139 N m: 0 / 13 / 40; 118 N m: 0 / 8 / 28; 89 N m: 0 / 6 / 2. By the rule (success: Fisher's exact test on the two counts, p below 0.01): at 139 N m: MPM still does better at +30°, not separated on the other slopes; at 118 N m: MPM still does better at +30°, not separated on the other slopes; at 89 N m: the advantage in success is gone. Velocity error over the trials that reach the goal (a difference counts from 0.05 m/s and two pooled standard deviations): at 139 N m MPM tracks better at +20°, +25°; at 118 N m MPM tracks better at +20°, +25°; at 89 N m MPM tracks better at no slope (at +20° MPM's error is lower by 0.048 m/s, 5.6 pooled standard deviations, just under the 0.05 m/s threshold; at +25° MPM's error is lower by 0.006 m/s, 0.5 pooled standard deviations). Not part of the rule: RFT itself at +30° reaches 8 of 40 at 118 N m (Fisher's exact test against its 13 of 40 at 139 N m: p = 0.31; a knee at the limit in 8 % of its samples on the slope), 6 of 40 at 89 N m (Fisher's exact test against its 13 of 40 at 139 N m: p = 0.11; a knee at the limit in 16 % of its samples on the slope); the drop is not shown. The cap is not neutral for RFT, though: in the trials where it succeeds without a cap RFT also depends on its knee torque for moments, exceeding 89 N m in 12 of its 13 trials that reach the goal (a median of 5 samples, 0.1 s, per trial; the ninth check's count, reproduced here), and the 89 N m cap binds for it as the share above shows. None of the three policies was trained with the lowered limit, so this says how these policies would do with a weaker knee, not which training method would be better if all were trained for it.

<b>Control (a): the same caps on the hip pitch joints instead of the knees.</b> Checked on 2026-11-25 (ninth check: numbers reproduced from the raw files, the rule preceded the runs; wording corrected as it asked). Reading committed before the runs (task record, definitions block, entry of 2026-11-25, 03:05). MPM policy, +30°, torque limit of the two hip pitch joints at the same shares of its configured 88 N m as the knee caps, knee and all other limits as configured; 40 trials per step with the seeds and starts of the knee runs, same sand, bed and bed setting. Every run reads the limit back from the simulation. Goals of 40 at 75 N m (0.85), 66 N m (0.75), 56 N m (0.64), 44 N m (0.50): 39, 40, 38, 8; the knee caps at the shares 0.85, 0.75, 0.64, 0.50 gave 28, 13, 2, 0. <b>The MPM policy's +30° climb is far more sensitive to the knee torque limit than to the hip pitch limit.</b> With the hip pitch at 0.75 and 0.64 of its limit it still reaches 40 and 38 of 40 goals (knee at the same shares: 13 and 2), and it fails only at 0.50 (8 of 40, 32 fall forward; the cap then binds in 73 % of the samples). By the rule fixed beforehand this counts as "not specific to the knee", because one binding hip pitch step fails; that is the rule's label, and the knee is special in degree. The step at 0.85 does not bind (torque at 95 % of the cap in 7 % of the samples, the rule asks for 10 %) and tests nothing. An equal share of the limit is not an equal intervention, and matched by how much of the uncapped torque is cut the knee is still the more sensitive joint: without any cap the MPM policy's torque is above 0.85, 0.75, 0.64 and 0.50 of the limit in 5, 11, 20, 44 % of the samples on the slope at the hip pitch and in 10, 13, 17, 25 % at the knee. Under the hip pitch caps that hold the walk does not get worse: velocity error of the trials that reach the goal 0.12, 0.11, 0.10 m/s against 0.13 m/s without a cap; sand under the sole per stance at the four steps 5.1, 4.9, 4.7, 4.2 cm, against 5.1 cm without a cap (under the knee caps it was 6.1, 7.1, 7.9 and 11.0 cm). A cap on one joint also changes what the policy does with the others, and neither outcome compares the policies.

<b>For the hardware test.</b> Configured limits of the leg joints in these runs: torque hip pitch 88, hip roll 139, hip yaw 88, knee 139, ankle pitch and roll 50 N m; speed 32, 20, 32, 20, 37, 37 rad/s. Unitree's description files list the same torques except the ankles (35 N m); its product page lists 90 N m (G1) and 120 N m (G1 EDU) for the knee. The simulation enforces the torque limits (the MuJoCo solver clamps the summed actuator force of a joint to that range) and does not enforce the speed limits (the solver has no joint speed limit, and there is no torque against speed curve). At +30° the MPM policy works at the knee's torque limit (95th percentile 0.98 of 139 N m, at or above 95 % of it in 8 % of the samples) while the knee moves at about 12 rad/s in those samples, and above the configured knee speed (95th percentile 23 rad/s, peaks 33 rad/s against 20 rad/s; the checker's values); a real motor does not give its peak torque at that speed. Its ankle pitch torque is at or above 95 % of 35 N m in 10 % of the samples on the slope at +30° (0.6 % at +25°). The checker also notes that its ankle roll rides the position limit. So "MPM reaches every goal at +30°" is a statement about the simulated actuator (139 N m at any joint speed, 50 N m ankles) and needs these limits stated beside it; the results at +20° and +25° do not depend on the knee limit down to 89 N m. The lowered knee limit and the 35 N m ankles were each run alone; the two together were not run.

<!-- T3-mixed-terrain:start -->
## Mixed MJWarp/MPM terrain task on the unified code (2026-11-20)

Purpose: the MPM policy `eayrj79r` overspeeds downhill (2.05 m/s at −30°, commanded 1.0 m/s) because the legacy
mixed training gave the sand robots only wave and random rough terrain. The new task gives every rough terrain
type once as rigid MJWarp ground and once as MPM sand, in one training run.

Nothing here is committed. All paths are relative to `~/isaac/worktrees/mpm-unification/IsaacLab` unless stated.

### Task IDs

- `IsaacContrib-Velocity-G1-29dof-MPM-Mixed` (training)
- `IsaacContrib-Velocity-G1-29dof-MPM-Mixed-Play`

Both use the standard PPO runner `G1MixedTerrainPPORunnerCfg` by default, as the legacy mixed training did.

### Design

| Item | How it works |
|---|---|
| Terrain | `ROUGH_TERRAINS_CFG` with 10 columns of 8 m. Each of the five terrain types fills two adjacent columns. |
| Ground per column | First column of each pair is rigid, second carries sand. The generated mesh is one rigid MJWarp collider everywhere; in sand columns the sand lies on top of it, so the sand top is one sand depth above the neighbouring rigid surface. |
| Which robots get sand | The first `sand_env_fraction` of the environments (default 0.5). Only these hold the `sand` asset; rigid environments hold no particles at all. This uses Isaac Lab clone combinations, as the legacy mixed task did. |
| Placement | Sand environments are spread over the sand columns, rigid ones over the rigid columns. With 10 environments: envs 0–4 on columns 1, 3, 5, 7, 9 and envs 5–9 on columns 0, 2, 4, 6, 8. With 2 environments both are on the wave pair. |
| Foot contact margin | 2 cm in sand environments, 0 cm in rigid environments, written per environment before the solvers are built. |
| Staying on the own ground | `root_out_of_patch` ends the episode as a time-out when the pelvis comes within 0.5 m of the side of its column or of the end of the tile grid. |
| Spawn | Robots start 5 cm higher than in the sand-only task, because the reset samples the ground height under the pelvis only. |
| MPM storage | Sized for the sand environments only: 41 M active cells at 4096 environments instead of 82 M. |
| Foot contact force | Sand reaction as in the sand-only task, plus the MJWarp ground contact force, so contact flags and gait timers work on rigid ground. |

The two columns of a pair have the same terrain type and the same difficulty band per row, but are not identical
copies: the generator draws the difficulty within the band, and the random rough heights, separately per cell.

### Files changed for the mixed task

| File | Change |
|---|---|
| `…/velocity_mpm/config/g1_29dof/streaming_mpm_mixed_env_cfg.py` (new, 182 lines) | `MixedMPMTerrainImporter`, `MixedMPMTerrainImporterCfg` (`sand_env_fraction`), `G1MixedTerminationsCfg`, `G1MPMMixedEnvCfg`, `G1MPMMixedEnvCfg_PLAY` |
| `…/velocity_mpm/config/g1_29dof/streaming_mpm_mixed_env.py` (new, 246 lines) | `G1MPMMixedEnv` (per-environment foot margin, rigid foot force, sand reset for sand environments), `MixedMPMObject`, `randomize_mpm_material` for sand environments |
| `…/g1_29dof/mdp/terminations.py`, `mdp/__init__.pyi` | New term `root_out_of_patch` |
| `…/g1_29dof/__init__.py` | Registers the two task IDs |
| `…/g1_29dof/env_cfg/terrain_generator_cfg.py` | `ROUGH_TERRAINS_CFG`: `num_cols` 5 → 10; random rough `noise_range` (0.02, 0.10) → (0.02, 0.08). Both also apply to the sand-only task. |
| `…/g1_29dof/streaming_mpm_env.py`, `streaming_mpm_env_cfg.py` | Removed the unused sampled foot margin (`foot_contact_margin_range` and its code). No mixed-task code in the base task. |
| Newton, `~/isaac/worktrees/mpm-unification/newton`: `newton/_src/solvers/implicit_mpm/particle_cache.py`, `particle_streaming.py` (+16 −5 lines) | The tile cache accepts worlds that hold no particles; such a world gets no pool. Needed for the cache presets on the mixed task. |

`velocity_mpm/config/deprecated/` is untouched.

### Config values

| Value | Default | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `scene.terrain.sand_env_fraction` | 0.5 | Share of environments on sand, in (0, 1]. A single environment is on sand when this is above 0.5. |
| `mpm_contact_margin` | 0.02 m | Foot margin in sand environments |
| `rigid_contact_margin` | 0.0 m | Foot margin in rigid environments |
| `terminations.out_of_patch.params.margin` | 0.5 m | Distance inside the patch edge at which the episode ends |
| Play reset range | ±3 m in x and y | Was ±5 m, which exceeds the 4 m half column |

### Commands

```
# Play: env 0 on sand, env 1 on rigid, both on the wave pair
uv run --with wandb isaaclab play --rl_library rsl_rl --task IsaacContrib-Velocity-G1-29dof-MPM-Mixed-Play \
    --num_envs 2 --viz newton_rtx --checkpoint wandb:jnskkmr/g1_29dof_mpm_mixed_terrain/eayrj79r \
    physics=solver_cfg_cache "env.video_recorders=[]"

# One robot on sand
... --num_envs 1 env.scene.terrain.sand_env_fraction=1.0
```

A scene with no sand environment (for example `--num_envs 1` with the default fraction) is rejected with a
config error; use `--num_envs 2` to see a rigid robot.

### Results, measured in this session on OSMO (policy `eayrj79r`)

Streaming without tile cache (`t3-mixed-nosand-1`):

| Case | Environments holding sand | Sand force on rigid robots' feet | Sand robots on their sand: foot force, falls |
|---|---|---|---|
| Play, 10 robots, 60 s, `solver_cfg_no_cache` | 0–4 | 0.0 N | 335 N, 0 |
| Training config, 10 robots, 120 s, `solver_cfg_all_active_no_cache` | 0–4 | 0.0 N | 328 N, 0 |
| Play, 2 robots, 30 s | 0 | 0.0 N | 348 N, 0 |
| Play, 1 robot, `sand_env_fraction=1.0`, 30 s | 0 | n/a | 348 N, 0 |

Streaming with the tile cache, after the Newton change (`t3-mixed-cache-1`, `solver_cfg_cache`):

| Case | Environments holding sand | Sand force on rigid robots' feet | Sand robots: foot force, falls | Time on the other ground |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Play, 2 robots, 30 s (the viewer command, headless) | 0 | 0.0 N | 339 N, 0 | 0 % |
| Play, 10 robots, 60 s | 0–4 | 0.0 N | 305 N, 0 | 0 % |
| Training config, 10 robots, 120 s | 0–4 | 0.0 N | 341 N, 0 | 0 % |
| Play, 1 robot, `sand_env_fraction=1.0`, 30 s | 0 | n/a | 340 N, 0 | 0 % |
| Sand-only task, Play, 10 robots, 30 s (regression check) | all | n/a | 341 N, 0 falls, 2 out-of-bounds resets | n/a |

`root_out_of_patch` with the tile-grid ends ran in this test: no robot was on the other ground at any step.

Earlier checks on the same task:

- Sand robots that stay on their sand behave as in the sand-only task (0 resets in 10 robots × 15 s for both;
  sole 17–18 cm below the surface in Play for both, where the sand is 0.35 m of loose poppy seed).
- A 0 cm foot margin on sand is clearly worse (2 resets in 10, body tilt 6–12° against 3–5°), which is why a
  robot must not cross onto the other ground.
- Before the time-out, robots spent 35 % of Play time (23 % with the training reset range) on the wrong ground.
  With the side-of-column time-out, rigid robots spent 0 % on sand.
- Force blow-ups of 100–700 kN on rigid random rough terrain came from 8 cm vertical walls; with
  `noise_range=(0.02, 0.08)` the largest foot force was 4.4 kN with no resets.

### Not verified yet

- The viewer (`--viz newton_rtx`) with the tile cache on the mixed task; all tests ran headless.
- No training has been launched on this task.

### Open issues and decisions

1. **Episodes cut at the patch border.** In the 120 s training-config tests, 41 of 77 episodes (side borders
   only) and 54 of 82 episodes (sides and tile-grid ends) ended at the patch border instead of running the full
   20 s. With alternating 8 m columns the border is at most 3.5 m away on both
   sides. The legacy layout (rigid half and sand half, five types repeated in each half) would leave a single
   dividing line; it needs the five sub-terrains listed twice in `ROUGH_TERRAINS_CFG`. Not changed.
2. **Trainings to launch.** Planned: `physics=solver_cfg_all_active_no_cache` and `physics=solver_cfg_cache`, to
   compare against `eayrj79r` (no cache). Waiting for the go-ahead and the GPU count; the legacy run used
   40 processes × 64 environments.
3. **Robot link collision type.** `MPMSolver.prepare_builder` turns every convex robot link into a triangle mesh
   in all MPM-coupled tasks. Suspected to make rigid contact harsher; untested, and it is shared library code.
4. **Newton changes are committed but not pushed** on branch `jnskkmhr/mpm-unification` of the Newton worktree:
   `01d6e3d3` (leak fix) and `e681453d` (tile cache with sand-free worlds).

<!-- T3-for-T1:start -->
## Notes for T1 from the mixed-terrain training (2026-11-21)

Found while launching `IsaacContrib-Velocity-G1-29dof-MPM-Mixed` trainings on OSMO (40 single-GPU L40 nodes x 64
environments, 32 of them on sand). The two Newton fixes are committed on branch `jnskkmhr/mpm-unification` of
`~/isaac/worktrees/mpm-unification/newton` (not pushed); the Isaac Lab changes are uncommitted.

### 1. Host-memory leak in the coupled solver (fixed in Newton commit `01d6e3d3`)

- **Symptom.** Training processes were killed with exit code 137 about 25 minutes after job start
  (`g1-mpm-mixed-cache-1` at iteration 12, `g1-mpm-mixed-no-cache-1` at iteration 36). Host memory of the training
  process grew about 4 GB per minute after the first iteration until it hit the 64 GB container limit. GPU memory
  was steady.
- **Cause.** `newton/_src/solvers/coupled/solver_coupled.py`. Every particle material change
  (`ModelFlags.MODEL_PROPERTIES`) makes the coupler project all particle attributes into each sub-solver view. For
  each attribute it builds a Python index list with one entry per particle (`_expanded_row_indices` copies the
  stored list) and `_indices_are_identity` keeps that list in `_identity_memo`, keyed by the list's `id`. A new
  list per call means a new entry per call, so the memo grew without bound. The memo was added in commit
  `fcd765f8` (2026-11-16).
- **Trigger.** Sand material randomization at reset (`mpm_material`, `mpm_material_log` events). It fires on
  every step in which an environment resets, which early in training is nearly every step.
- **Not affected.** Tasks without material randomization at reset: the speed-bench tasks and the tow tasks.
  Measured on one GPU with 64 environments: the sand-only walking task leaks at the same rate as the mixed task;
  with the two material events switched off it does not leak (host memory flat at 7-9 GB).
- **Not the cause.** The viewer and video recording, W&B, thread limits (`OMP_NUM_THREADS` and similar), the
  number of terrain columns, the mixed-task code.
- **Fix.** 7 lines in `solver_coupled.py`: `_expanded_row_indices` returns the stored list for row width 1
  instead of a copy, and `_identity_memo` is cleared when it reaches 256 entries. A CPU regression test
  (`test_model_properties_notifications_do_not_accumulate_index_lists` in `newton/tests/test_coupled_solver.py`)
  passes with the fix and fails without it (the memo grows from 50 to 175 entries over five notifications).
- **Result with the fix** (one GPU, 64 environments, material randomization on): host memory flat at 6-7.5 GB
  over 30 iterations without cache and at about 10 GB with cache.

### 2. Iteration time, unified against legacy

Earlier in this record the unified task was compared with the legacy run `eayrj79r` using its whole-run average of
10.6 s per iteration. That comparison was wrong: iteration time depends strongly on the training stage, because
resets are expensive and episodes are short at the start.

Legacy run `eayrj79r` (legacy moving patch without cache, 40 nodes x 64 environments, from W&B):

| Iterations | Mean episode length [steps] | Collection time [s] | Learning time [s] |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0-50 | 42 | 18.2 | 5.4 |
| 50-500 | 75 | 16.8 | 5.0 |
| 500-5000 | 509 | 8.5 | 3.3 |
| 5000-20000 | 902 | 6.6 | 2.6 |

Unified mixed task at the same stage (iterations 3-29, mean episode length about 44 steps, one GPU, 64 environments
of which 32 on sand, material randomization on, with the leak fix):

| Preset | Collection time [s] | Learning time [s] |
|---|---|---|
| `solver_cfg_no_cache` | 12.5 | 0.13 |
| `solver_cfg_cache` | 16.4 (40-43 while the first video is recorded) | 0.13 |
| `solver_cfg_no_cache`, before the leak fix | about 27 per iteration in total | |

- At the same training stage the unified streaming task without cache collects faster than the legacy run
  (12.5 s against 18.2 s). It is not slower.
- The learning time is not comparable: the unified numbers are from one node, the legacy numbers include the
  gradient synchronization across 40 nodes.
- **With the tile cache the mixed task is slower than without it** in this setting (16.4 s against 12.5 s
  collection). The cache preset holds 50,176 particle slots per sand environment against 20,000 without cache.
  This is early training with a reset about every 44 steps; it has not been measured for long episodes.
- The speed-bench configuration notes the same cost from the other side: with material randomization at reset an
  iteration took 35-45 s with 32 environments and 5 s without. That measurement predates the leak fix, so part of
  it was the leak.

### 3. Tile cache with worlds that hold no particles (Newton commit `e681453d`)

The mixed task gives sand only to the sand environments. `newton/_src/solvers/implicit_mpm/particle_cache.py`
required the same number of simulation slots in every world and raised otherwise. Changed (16 lines added, 5
removed, with `particle_streaming.py`): a world without particles gets no pool, and the equal-slots check applies
only to worlds that hold particles. Regression check on the sand-only task with `solver_cfg_cache`: 10 robots,
30 s, same sinkage and speed as before.

### 4. OSMO launcher for the unified stack

`osmo/run_multinode.sh` and `osmo/osmo_multi_gpu.yaml` were copied from `~/isaac/IsaacLab/osmo` into the unified
checkout and adapted (30 changed lines): the unified `uv.lock` installs Newton and Warp from the sibling folders
`../newton` and `../warp`, so the launcher uploads those folders and the workflow copies them next to the code.
It also copies the git-ignored robot `.usda` assets. Usage is unchanged.

### 5. Running trainings

`g1-mpm-mixed-cache-3` (`physics=solver_cfg_cache`, run name `train_scratch_cache`) and `g1-mpm-mixed-no-cache-3`
(`physics=solver_cfg_no_cache`, run name `train_scratch_no_cache`), W&B project `g1_29dof_mpm_mixed_terrain`,
submitted 2026-11-21 on `isaac-lab-l40s-03` with the fixes above. A four-node probe on that pool trained at
12.7-13.0 s per iteration without cache, the same as on L40.
<!-- T3-for-T1:end -->

## Requested changes to `tasks/T3.md`, for the lead session (2026-11-21)

`tasks/T3.md` is edited by the lead session only, so the changes Junnosuke decided in the T3 session on
2026-11-21 are recorded here. Items marked "decided" are his; items marked "suggested" are from the T3 session and
need his or the lead's confirmation.

### 1. Fold P4 into P1 and P2 (decided)

P4 ("Where do particles not help?", the P1 metrics on rigid ground, step 5) stops being a row of its own. The
question is answered inside P1 and P2, as an analysis along two directions:

- **Terrain geometry (in P1).** On flat and gently sloped ground every policy succeeds, so training on particles
  does not matter much there. It starts to matter on steeper slopes and for walking that is not straight.
- **Terrain material (in P2).** On hard ground training on particles does not matter much. It starts to matter as
  the ground gets softer: the baselines' tracking error rises and their step time shortens, while the MPM policy
  stays steady (the density-friction grid figure, Fig. 4 of the T3 slides).

Requested edits:

| Where in `tasks/T3.md` | Change |
|---|---|
| Acceptance table, row P1 | Add: "Report where the policies do not differ (flat and gentle slopes) as well as where they do." Add the walking directions of item 2 below. |
| Acceptance table, row P2 | Add: "Report along the material axis from hard to soft, including hard ground where the policies do not differ." Add step time to the metrics. |
| Acceptance table, row P4 | Remove. |
| Steps, step 5 ("Rigid ground") | Remove as a step of its own; rigid ground becomes the hard end of the material axis in step 3. |
| Slides list, "P4: the same policies on rigid ground" | Remove. |
| Row P5 | Renumber if the lead wants consecutive numbers. |

### 2. Walking directions on slopes (decided)

P1 so far evaluates straight walking only. Add sideways, diagonal and circular walking on slopes on top of straight
walking. Not implemented yet. Before fixing the commands, the T3 session will check each policy's training command
ranges so that every evaluated command is inside all three.

### 3. Axes and what each depends on (decided)

| Axis | Content | Depends on |
|---|---|---|
| P1 | Generalization to rougher terrain: slopes, and walking directions on slopes | The MPM policy retrained on every terrain type (item 5) |
| P2 | Performance under different terrain material, from hard to soft | The same policies |
| P3 | Soil interaction with an object | T2's object towing. `tasks/T3.md` still says "object pushing"; T2's task is now towing. |

### 4. Hardware (decided, with one suggestion)

- Hardware remains the deciding comparison and applies to P1 to P3.
- For the initial submission what hardware can show is limited: on flat ground and on a 10 degree slope every
  policy succeeded. Step times Junnosuke measured on hardware (flat / 10 degrees): MPM 0.33 / 0.34 s,
  RFT 0.42 / 0.39 s, MJWarp 0.39 / 0.36 s.
- Decided: try a steeper slope, about 20 degrees, to see whether a gap appears.
- Suggested, from the simulated sweep below: 20 degrees should separate MPM from MJWarp on success and from RFT on
  tracking error; separating RFT on success needs about 30 degrees, so 25 degrees is probably the practical upper
  end on dry sand. A downhill test with the current MPM policy would look bad (see item 5).

Simulated uphill sweep on MPM sand at 1.0 m/s, 10 trials per cell, measured in this session (MPM `eayrj79r`,
RFT `6t0h2ud8`, MJWarp `3qymp37c`):

| Slope | MJWarp goals | RFT goals | MPM goals | Velocity error MJWarp / RFT / MPM [m/s] |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| +10 deg | 10 | 10 | 10 | 0.07 / 0.10 / 0.05 |
| +15 deg | 10 | 10 | 10 | 0.08 / 0.11 / 0.06 |
| +20 deg | 7 | 10 | 10 | 0.13 / 0.13 / 0.06 |
| +25 deg | 6 | 10 | 10 | 0.21 / 0.24 / 0.08 |
| +30 deg | 6 | 4 | 10 | 0.32 / 0.31 / 0.12 |

### 5. MPM-Rough is being retrained (decided)

- Reason: the current MPM policy `eayrj79r` came from the legacy mixed training, where the sand robots only saw
  wave and random rough terrain; both pyramid slopes and flat were rigid only. Downhill on sand it tracks worst
  (velocity error 0.52 m/s at -20 degrees against 0.10 m/s for both baselines) and reaches 7 of 10 goals at
  -30 degrees.
- New training: `IsaacContrib-Velocity-G1-29dof-MPM-Mixed` on the unified code, every terrain type once as rigid
  ground and once as sand. Rewards unchanged from `eayrj79r`, so that a difference can be attributed to terrain
  coverage. Details in "Mixed MJWarp/MPM terrain task on the unified code" above.
- Jobs, submitted 2026-11-21 on `isaac-lab-l40s-03`, 40 nodes x 64 environments, 20,000 iterations:
  `g1-mpm-mixed-cache-3` (`physics=solver_cfg_cache`, run name `train_scratch_cache`) and
  `g1-mpm-mixed-no-cache-3` (`physics=solver_cfg_no_cache`, run name `train_scratch_no_cache`), W&B project
  `g1_29dof_mpm_mixed_terrain`. Result pending; `eayrj79r` took 59 hours.
- `tasks/T3.md` says "MPM-Rough is the policy retrained in T2 step 1". The lead should say which run is MPM-Rough
  once these finish.

### 6. Points the lead should know when rewriting the task (from the T3 session)

- **RFT baseline.** RFT-Rough is `6t0h2ud8` (rigid support 0 to 0.40 m below the sand). Junnosuke decided on
  2026-11-20 not to add a traction heuristic to RFT.
- **P2 does not separate the policies on success.** All three reached the goal on 50 of 50 random sands and 210
  of 210 grid sands. The separation is in tracking error (MPM 0.08 m/s, RFT 0.12 m/s) and in step time. P2's
  metrics in the task file (success rate, pelvis jerk, joint smoothness) should include these two.
- **Baselines are not from one commit.** `3qymp37c` and `6t0h2ud8` were trained in the old worktree, not from the
  unified commit the task file requires. Not decided whether to retrain them.
- **Terrain shapes outside the training set** (P1) have not been evaluated; only slopes.
- **Rigid-ground results are incomplete.** Step time on rigid ground was measured with the old RFT policy, not
  `6t0h2ud8`, and the rigid downhill evaluation is broken (every policy 0 of 10 at -10 degrees). Both need
  redoing before rigid ground is used as the hard end of the material axis.
- **Pelvis-height reward on sand.** The target is 0.50 m above the sand surface against 0.75 m on rigid ground,
  in `eayrj79r` and in the new trainings. Junnosuke decided on 2026-11-21 to leave it unchanged for this
  comparison.

### Goal reaching

Goal: the robot's path (its horizontal speed summed over time) reaches 10 m before the time limit (40 s at 0.5 m/s, 20 s at 1 m/s); it is a path length, not a distance from the start. Fall: any termination, which is a pelvis tilt above 45° or the pelvis-height term. Time limit: trials that neither fell nor reached the goal. Time to goal: mean ± std over the trials that reached it. The remaining columns are mean ± std over the trials without a fall, each averaged from 5 s to its end (after the start-up of the tow), and empty when fewer than three trials stayed up. Forward speed error: mean absolute difference between the base-frame forward velocity and the command; the base frame leans with the pelvis, so the robot speed (horizontal, world frame) is given beside it. Yaw-rate error: mean absolute difference between the instantaneous base yaw rate and the command; it includes the yaw oscillation of each step. Tether tension and horizontal tether pull are the magnitude and the horizontal part of the same logged tether force; averaged over a trial the horizontal force of the ground on the cube equals the pull, so these are one measurement, not two. The cube speed is given because the cube does not move at the commanded speed on every ground. Net distance: horizontal distance between the start and the end of the trial, for the robot and for the cube, over the trials without a fall; because the goal counts path, a robot can reach it while the cube has moved little, so the goals with a net cube travel under 8 m are counted beside it (threshold fixed before computing). Cube lower than at rest: height of the cube while it rests before the tether goes taut (0.2 to 2 s, tension below 5 N, speed below 0.02 m/s) minus its height while towed, per trial; it needs no surface level. With the turning command the robot walks a circle of 1 m radius, shorter than the 1.2 m tether, so the cube stays near the centre and pivots: those rows test turning while tied to the cube, not dragging it along a curve.

### Sweep over sands from stiff to soft

The three final tow policies on rigid ground and on 13 MPM sands: friction 0.7 and 0.9 at densities from 250 to 3,000 kg/m³, plus beach sand and packed and loose poppy seed. Command 0.5 m/s straight, 15 kg cube attached (goal 10 m of path, limit 40 s) and tether cut (20 s), 16 trials per cell (2 runs of 8, seeds 42 and 43), start as in training. Bed 0.6 m deep, voxel 0.04 m, all particles awake, sand template settled for 12 s; each sand is settled as itself and every trial starts on its settled surface (pelvis 0.76 m above it). Design fixed before running: `outputs/T2.md`, block T2-SANDS. OSMO `t2-tow-sands-1` on NVIDIA L40; IsaacLab `0007b2e67` with 76 uncommitted entries, Newton `48231a989` clean. Rigid ground is from the earlier runs. Unchecked.

Takeaway: For the MPM-trained policy the torso pitch and the tether tension rise together from stiff to soft ground as a rank correlation (+0.84 over 13 grounds, +0.93 over the 11 at equal cube speed; over the sands alone +0.79), from 8.5° at 60 N on rigid ground to 15 to 20° at 83 to 106 N on sand. It is not monotonic and is not evidence that the policy scales its lean to the pull: the pitch saturates near 20°, where the lean penalty starts (17 and 19° at 126 and 140 N on the two softest sands), and with the tether cut the pitch already rises with softness, from 9° to 15° (dashed line). The cube adds 5 to 8° on ten sands and 3 to 4° on the two softest, and that extra pitch does not follow the tension. The two baselines give too few grounds for a correlation.
Takeaway: On the 7 stiffer MPM sands the RFT-trained policy stays up with the tether cut (110 of 112 trials) but reaches the goal with the 15 kg cube in 12 of 112: it pitches forward and falls, on average 2.5 to 7.2 s into the trial per sand (single falls 1.5 to 13.7 s). The tether tension on these sands is 83 to 92 N, against 60 N on rigid ground and 73 N on RFT sand, where the same policy tows in 16 of 16. Caveat: it is already a marginal walker on these sands without the cube (0.63 to 0.71 m/s at a 0.5 m/s command, torso pitch 13 to 15° against 6° on rigid ground), so these runs alone do not separate a tension that is too high from a policy close to falling forward that any tether load tips; the control with lighter and heavier cubes in the next section shows it is not the tension alone: the same policy stays up at 83 to 98 N on rigid ground and RFT sand, and on MPM sand it already falls in up to 5 of 16 trials at 56 to 60 N. The rigid-trained policy does not walk on any of the sands. The MPM-trained policy stays up and reaches the goal in 16 of 16 on all 12; on the softest of them (250 kg/m³, friction 0.9) the cube moves at 0.36 m/s and in 5 of the 16 goal trials it has travelled less than 8 m when the robot's path reaches 10 m, so 11 of 16 count as transport there and 16 of 16 on the other 11.

Figures [tow_sands_pitch.png](../site/T3/fig/tow_sands_pitch.png) and [tow_sands_outcome.png](../site/T3/fig/tow_sands_outcome.png).

### Baselines retrained to walk without the object

The earlier baseline tow policies fell on MPM sand even without the cube, so their failure said nothing about the cube. Both were retrained from their walking policies on those policies' rough terrain, with the cube's mass drawn from 1 to 15 kg and a quarter of the environments towing nothing (MJWarp: OSMO g1-tow-rough-rigid-1, W&B bc1o99un; RFT: g1-tow-rough-rft-1, W&B 5x48qh9t; 20,000 iterations each). They are tested like the sweep: 12 MPM sands, command 0.5 m/s, 16 trials per cell, tether cut and 8, 10 and 15 kg cubes (OSMO t2-tow-base2-1, logs logs/t2/tow_base2), and afterwards 2 and 5 kg cubes (OSMO t2-tow-base2-light-1, same folder; these two are unchecked); MPM is the existing policy her588wk. A goal counts only with 8 m or more of cube travel. Design, rules and recipe differences fixed before training: outputs/T2.md, block T2-BASELINES2. Checked independently, pass with caveats (checks/T2.md, check 6): the counts, speeds and tensions below reproduce; the follow-up runs named at the end are not in yet and are unchecked.

![Trials without a fall with the tether cut, and trials that transport the 15 kg cube, per policy and sand](fig/tow_baselines_outcome.png) Per tow policy and MPM sand, from stiff to soft: share of 16 trials without a fall with the tether cut (hatched) and share of 16 trials that reach the goal with the 15 kg cube having travelled 8 m or more (filled). Command 0.5 m/s.
<div class="callout"><b>Takeaway:</b> Without the cube the retrained RFT baseline walks on 10 of 12 MPM sands and the retrained MJWarp baseline on 8 (15 or 16 of 16 trials up at 0.40 to 0.47 m/s); with the 15 kg cube neither transports it on any sand, while the MPM policy does on all 12 (11 of 16 trials on the softest).</div>

![Share of trials against the load on the tether, per policy, over the 12 sands](fig/tow_baselines_load.png) Over the 12 sands: share of trials without a fall with the tether cut, and share of trials that transport the cube (8 m or more of cube travel) with 2, 5, 8, 10 and 15 kg. The 2 and 5 kg points are unchecked.
<div class="callout"><b>Takeaway:</b> On the sands where they walk, the retrained baselines transport the 2 kg cube in every trial and the 5 kg cube in most (tether at 24 to 32 N, cube slowed to 0.21 to 0.31 m/s); from 8 kg on they stay upright and step in place and no trial moves the cube 8 m, while on rigid ground the same policies tow every cube up to 15 kg in 16 of 16.</div>

Reading by the rule fixed beforehand ("walks without the object": at least 14 of 16 up with the tether cut at 0.40 to 0.60 m/s; then the 15 kg cube with 8 m of travel decides): the retrained MJWarp baseline fails only with the object on 8 sands and fails anyway on 4 (the four softest, where it falls without the cube); the retrained RFT baseline fails only with the object on 10 and fails anyway on 2; the MPM policy does not fail on 11 and by the rule fails only with the object on the softest sand (it stays up, 11 of 16 trials move the cube 8 m). For the MJWarp baseline the clean case "walks without the cube, stalls with it" is seven sands; on the eighth, light sand, it falls with every cube.

What the failure is (stride and foot measures from the independent check 6, checks/T2.md): the baselines stop striding. On the sands where they walk, in 51 of 54 cells they stay up for the whole trial with the cube at 0.01 to 0.13 m/s and the tether at 29 to 48 N. While stalled they keep stepping at their usual rate but the leg sweeps 2 to 6 cm per stance instead of 22 cm without the cube, the torso pitch is 6 to 8 degrees (the 20 degree lean band is not used), and the horizontal force on the feet is 22 to 28 N whatever the cube's mass; for moments their tension reaches 78 to 92 N. The MPM policy on the same sands leans 13 to 21 degrees, and its feet take 33 to 90 N with 2% of the leg sweep or less lost to foot travel. So this is not a limit of the sand under the feet and not a limit of the force the robots can produce.

What can be said about the resistive force: the object resists more on MPM sand than on rigid ground (8 kg: 42 to 47 N against 32 N; 10 kg: 54 to 60 N against 40 N; 15 kg: 83 to 92 N against 59 N; sand values from the MPM policy on the seven stiffer sands), and there the baselines develop only 29 to 48 N, upright and stepping in place. The larger resistance alone is not the explanation: the 10 kg cube on sand needs 54 to 60 N, which is what both baselines deliver with the 15 kg cube on rigid ground (58 to 59 N, 16 of 16), yet on sand they tow the 10 kg cube in 0 of 16. The 15 kg cells also ask the baselines for more pull than they met in training (about 59 N for MJWarp, about 72 N for RFT), so the 8 and 10 kg cells are the ones that carry the comparison. On RFT sand with the MPM sand's values the retrained RFT baseline shows the same behaviour with 10 and 15 kg (0 of 16, 39 to 41 N) and tows 8 kg in 7 of 16; it was trained with the RFT default material on the cube.

Acceptance test (walks without the object on the five named sands): the RFT baseline passes; the MJWarp baseline does not, it falls in 16 of 16 on loose poppy seed. As fixed beforehand, one variation of the MJWarp baseline with half of the environments towing nothing is training (OSMO g1-tow-rough-rigid-half-1) and will be reported beside this one. Lighter cubes (unchecked): the heaviest cube the baselines transport on MPM sand is 5 kg. With 2 kg both transport it in 16 of 16 on every sand where they walk (MJWarp 8 sands, RFT 10), at 0.35 to 0.41 m/s. With 5 kg the RFT baseline transports it in 13 to 16 of 16 on its 10 sands and the MJWarp baseline in 16 of 16 on six sands, 10 of 16 on packed poppy seed (six run out of time) and 9 of 16 on light sand (seven fall); the cube moves at 0.21 to 0.31 m/s under 24 to 32 N, against 0.39 to 0.44 m/s under 20 N on rigid ground. The MPM policy tows both cubes at 0.55 to 0.59 m/s on all 12 sands. So the baselines' tow on sand slows from 5 kg and stops by 8 kg, between about 30 N and the 42 to 47 N the 8 kg cube needs there. An MJWarp baseline trained with cubes up to 25 kg is still training (OSMO g1-tow-rough-rigid-heavy-1), to remove the load range as a confound.

![Slide and sink of the stance sole per stance against the peak horizontal ground force on the foot](fig/tow_pushoff.png) Does the sand give way under the baselines' push-off? Per loaded stance (vertical foot force above 165 N), 8 and 10 kg cubes pooled: how far the sole moves back (left) and how far its lowest corner sinks (right) from loading to unloading, against the peak horizontal ground force on that foot; medians in eight bins of force. Solid: dense sand, beach sand and packed poppy seed pooled; dotted: loose poppy seed alone (the MJWarp baseline falls there); dashed: rigid ground with the same cubes. The axis ends at 190 N; the last bins of the MJWarp baseline on rigid ground and of the MPM policy on the stiffer sands lie at or beyond it. Checked independently, pass with caveats (checks/T2.md, check 7); the numbers here are recomputed after the check with its corrections. Reading fixed before computing (outputs/T2.md, block T2-PUSHOFF).
<div class="callout"><b>Takeaway:</b> On MPM sand the ground under the baselines' feet moves in every stance (soles creep 1 to 1.5 cm and sink 1 to 2 cm after loading; rigid ground 0.1 cm and none), but it moves about as much without the cube and gives no more when the tension rises.</div>

Junnosuke's proposed explanation, "on MPM the terrain is more slippery and penetrates, so it is hard to earn enough repulsion force to push the object", tested on the existing rollouts by the rule fixed beforehand: mixed. The sand does slide and yield under the feet; that this denies the baselines the reaction force is not shown on dense sand, beach sand and packed poppy seed.

- The ground moves. Per loaded stance (medians) the baselines' soles move back 1.0 to 1.5 cm (MJWarp 1.5, RFT 1.0 to 1.2) and sink a further 0.9 to 2.2 cm after the foot is loaded, against 0.0 to 0.1 cm and no sink on rigid ground, at the same mean horizontal foot force (22 to 34 N against 25 to 27 N). By the check's count the feet stand 2.6 to 6.5 cm below the settled surface at the end of a stance. The comparison is of stepping in place on sand with walking at 0.4 m/s on rigid ground.
- It moves without any push. With the tether cut, walking the same sands at 0.41 to 0.44 m/s with no net horizontal foot force, the same baselines' soles move back 0.7 to 0.9 cm and sink 2.3 to 4.0 cm per stance: about half of the slide and all of the sink are there without the cube.
- It does not give more with the push by the amount fixed beforehand (1 cm). From the weakest to the strongest fifth of peak horizontal foot force the slide grows by 0.1 to 0.2 cm for the MJWarp baseline (rank correlation +0.03 to +0.11) and by 0.4 to 0.8 cm for the RFT baseline (+0.25 to +0.37), and the sink falls by 0.1 to 0.9 cm (-0.27 to -0.01). In the 0.3 s after the tether tension rises through its 90th percentile (47 to 54 N) the loaded foot moves back 0.1 to 0.3 cm and sinks 0.0 to 0.2 cm, as in any 0.3 s of stance (0.2 to 0.3 and 0.0 to 0.2 cm).
- Exception: on loose poppy seed the RFT baseline's feet do give more with force (rank correlation +0.60 with slide and +0.50 with sink; 2.3 cm more slide and 4.6 cm more sink); the MJWarp baseline falls there.
- What the baselines do there: they step in place and pull 24 to 31 N, where the 8 and 10 kg cubes need 40 to 45 N of horizontal pull, at a horizontal-to-vertical foot force ratio of 0.11 to 0.14 at the stance peak (0.17 to 0.22 on rigid ground). On rigid ground with the 15 kg cube they keep their speed through tension peaks of 70 to 72 N.
- The MPM policy on the same three sands sets its toes in: the lowest sole corner goes 2.2 to 4.6 cm down per stance, deeper the harder it pushes (rank correlation +0.47 to +0.52), while the sole centre sinks 0.1 to 2.6 cm and no deeper with force (-0.23 to +0.02); its soles move back 0.5 to 0.8 cm. It takes a peak horizontal foot force of 75 to 77 N per stance against the baselines' 42 to 59 N, 1.3 to 1.8 times sand by sand, at a force ratio of 0.18 to 0.19.

Not shown, and not to be read from this: that the sand cannot give the reaction force, that the feet slip when the baselines push, or that the baselines respond to rising tension or to sinking by reducing their push (the tension peaks are lurches of a robot that is already stalled, and the foot force also falls after such peaks on rigid ground). Nothing is claimed about lean: at equal pull the baselines lean as much on sand as on rigid ground, and the pelvis-offset measure has more slack than any shortfall it shows. Why the baselines' foot force stays low on sand cannot be read from these logs (no actions or joint positions were logged; the sand displaced under the sole is not saved).

Two definitions were changed after a first pass over the data: the sink is measured at the lowest sole corner instead of the sole centre (a first result existed when this was changed; both are now tabulated), and after the check the slide is measured along the robot's heading at the start of the stance, so that a turn during the stance does not count as slide.

### Range of tether tension per terrain solver

Which range of tether tension does each terrain solver give when its terrain parameters are changed? MPM-trained tow policy, 15 kg cube, command 0.5 m/s, 16 trials per terrain (2 runs of 8, seeds 42 and 43), tension as the mean from 5 s over the trials that stay up. Rigid ground (MJWarp): ground and cube friction 0.3 to 1.0. RFT sand: the 13 density and friction pairs of the MPM sands, surface friction equal to internal friction. MPM sand: the 12 usable sands of the sweep. Rigid ground and RFT sand run locally on one RTX 5090 (logs logs/t2/tow_force_range); design fixed before running: outputs/T2.md, block T2-FORCE-RANGE. Unchecked.

![Tether tension per terrain solver with side views of the robot](fig/tow_force_range.png) Tether tension the MPM-trained tow policy meets on each terrain solver as the terrain parameters are varied: band from the lowest to the highest terrain, one point per terrain, line at the mean. Open points: the cube moves slower than 0.45 m/s. Insets: side views of the robot from single-environment renderings of the same policy, at the frame whose torso pitch equals that terrain's mean (rigid ground with friction 0.5, MPM beach sand, MPM 250 kg/m³ with friction 0.9). The paper version is figures/t3_tow_force_v3.pdf.

What it shows: MPM sand gives the highest tension, 83 to 140 N (mean 99 N), against 41 to 93 N on rigid ground (mean 70 N) and 57 to 75 N on RFT sand (mean 66 N). What it does not show: that MPM gives the wider range, or the force RFT provides at 0.5 m/s. Rigid ground reaches 93 N by friction alone and overlaps seven of the twelve MPM sands; at equal cube speed (0.45 to 0.55 m/s) rigid ground spans 41 to 93 N and MPM sand 83 to 106 N (ten sands), the two softest MPM sands being slower. On RFT sand the MPM-trained policy drags the cube at 0.12 to 0.22 m/s on twelve sands (0.43 m/s on the thirteenth), so no RFT point is at equal speed.

RFT sand towed by the RFT-trained policy, for an RFT tension at about 0.5 m/s (same 13 sands and settings, logs logs/t2/tow_force_range_rftpolicy; not in the figure): it tows on six sands, 72.0 to 73.0 N with the cube at 0.31 to 0.46 m/s (three of them at equal cube speed, 0.455 to 0.462 m/s: 72.3 to 72.9 N; a fourth is at 0.4496 m/s, 72.7 N), and stalls on the seven denser or higher-friction ones (cube 0.06 to 0.12 m/s, 60 to 64 N, 0 of 16 goals, no fall). At equal cube speed the three solvers are then: rigid ground 41 to 93 N, RFT sand 72 to 73 N, MPM sand 83 to 106 N.

Tension is the magnitude of the tether force; the tether rises from the cube to the pelvis, so the tension is 9 to 21% above its horizontal part, which is what the ground resists the cube with. Horizontal force at equal cube speed and cube friction 0.5: 50 N on rigid ground, 62 N on RFT sand (RFT-trained policy), 69 to 94 N on the ten MPM sands at equal speed, that is 1.1 to 1.5 times RFT and 1.4 to 1.9 times rigid ground (loose poppy seed: 94 N, 1.5 and 1.9 times). On the softest MPM sand (140 N, open point) the tow is still slowing at the end of the trial, so that value is not a steady one (independent check 5). An alternative version of the paper figure, figures/t3_tow_force_v4.pdf, shows the RFT-trained policy's six towed sands in the RFT column instead of the MPM-trained policy's drag points. RFT surface friction, one sand (640 / 0.73, RFT-trained policy, 16 of 16 goals each; logs logs/t2/tow_force_range_rftsurface): tension 67.7 N with surface friction 0.3 (horizontal 57.7 N, cube 0.47 m/s), 72.6 N with 0.5 and 72.7 N with 0.73 (horizontal 62.2 and 62.3 N, cube 0.45 m/s). So the pull rises by 5 N from 0.3 to 0.5 and not at all above 0.5: the RFT pull on this cube saturates.

- MJWarp: 8 of 8 terrains give a point; tension 41 to 93 N, mean 70 N; cube speed 0.46 to 0.50 m/s; at equal cube speed (0.45 to 0.55 m/s) 8 terrains, 41 to 93 N, mean 70 N.
- RFT: 13 of 13 terrains give a point; tension 57 to 75 N, mean 66 N; cube speed 0.12 to 0.43 m/s; at equal cube speed (0.45 to 0.55 m/s) 0 terrains.
- MPM: 12 of 13 terrains give a point; tension 83 to 140 N, mean 99 N; cube speed 0.36 to 0.52 m/s; at equal cube speed (0.45 to 0.55 m/s) 10 terrains, 83 to 106 N, mean 92 N.

### Is it the tension or the ground? Towing with lighter and heavier cubes

Does the RFT-trained policy's outcome follow the tether tension or the ground? The same 0.3 m cube with other masses, RFT-trained and MPM-trained tow policies, command 0.5 m/s, 16 trials per cell (2 runs of 8, seeds 42 and 43), goal 10 m of path, limit 40 s. Lighter cube (8 and 10 kg) on the 7 MPM sands where the RFT-trained policy walks without the cube, with the settings of the sweep (OSMO t2-tow-sands-mass-1, NVIDIA L40, logs logs/t2/tow_sands_ctrl). Heavier cube on rigid ground (21 and 24 kg) and on RFT sand with the MPM sand's values (18 and 20 kg), run locally on one RTX 5090 (logs logs/t2/tow_heavy). The 15 kg rows are the earlier runs. Design and reading rule fixed before running: outputs/T2.md, block T2-SANDS-CTRL. Unchecked.

Result: mixed, and closer to the ground than to the tension. On rigid ground and RFT sand the RFT-trained policy does not fall at 83 to 98 N; there it gives way as the load grows, the cube slowing to 0.43 and 0.33 m/s on rigid ground and 0.36 and 0.29 m/s on RFT sand (16, 11, 16 and 16 of 16 goals; the 5 misses at 95 N on rigid ground are trials that ran out of time). On MPM sand it does not slow down, and it falls at a tension it carries elsewhere: at 56 to 60 N with the cube at 0.75 to 0.80 m/s (10 kg cube; 60 N is the tension it tows at on rigid ground with 15 kg, at 0.48 m/s) it reaches the goal in 16, 15, 11, 13, 11 and 11 of 16 on the six sands in the target band, and with the 8 kg cube at 41 to 47 N in 15 or 16 of 16 on all seven. The tension on MPM sand is the same within 2 N at both policies' cube speeds, so the higher speed does not inflate it. On MPM sand its outcome does follow the load (8 kg: 111 of 112 goals; 10 kg: 92 of 112; 15 kg: 12 of 112), but the load with which it stays up there is far lower than on rigid ground or RFT sand. With the 8 and 10 kg cubes it tows faster than commanded on MPM sand (cube at 0.66 to 0.80 m/s for a 0.5 m/s command) with a torso pitch of 21 to 28 degrees; with 15 kg the few trials that stay up are at 0.77 to 0.86 m/s and 31 to 34 degrees. The MPM-trained policy tows the same cubes at 0.53 to 0.56 m/s with 14 to 17 degrees and reaches the goal in 16 of 16 in every sand cell.

Supported reading (from the independent check, numbers recomputed here in the table's last columns): on MPM sand the RFT-trained policy walks faster than commanded and pitched forward, so a tow load it carries elsewhere tips it over. Its largest pelvis tilt in the first 5 s, mean over the trials that stay up, is 22 to 29 degrees without the cube, 31 to 38 degrees with 10 kg and 41 to 44 degrees with 15 kg on the seven MPM sands, against 9 to 17 degrees on rigid ground and RFT sand; a trial ends at 45 degrees. The margin is small without the cube and the load uses it up. A fall here means crossing that 45 degree limit in a forward lurch; whether each such trial would have ended on the ground is not in the data. Trials of different cells share their start states (same seeds), so the falls are not independent between cells.

By the rule fixed beforehand: one heavy-cube cell is inside the 85 to 95 N band (RFT sand, 18 kg, 88 N: succeeds); the two rigid-ground cells are just outside it (83.4 and 95.3 N). Six light-cube cells are inside the 55 to 65 N band: the RFT-trained policy succeeds (at least 14 of 16) in 2 and fails in 4. The MPM-trained policy does not tow on RFT sand: with 18 and 20 kg it is counted as reaching the goal in 12 of 16, but the goal is 10 m of robot path, and in those trials the cube moved 4.4 and 3.6 m at 0.12 and 0.10 m/s; with 15 kg the cube moved 6.0 m. These are listed under the table as goal cells without real transport.

Reading by the rule fixed beforehand, RFT-trained policy:

### What the numbers show

- trained on rigid reaches the goal in 48 of 48 on rigid ground, 4 of 48 on RFT sand, 0 of 48 on MPM sand.
- trained on rigid + RFT reaches the goal in 48 of 48 on rigid ground, 48 of 48 on RFT sand, 0 of 48 on MPM sand.
- trained on rigid + MPM reaches the goal in 48 of 48 on rigid ground, 43 of 48 on RFT sand, 48 of 48 on MPM sand.
- Trials without a fall with the tether cut, 0.5 m/s command, on rigid ground: trained on rigid 16 of 16, trained on rigid + RFT 16 of 16, trained on rigid + MPM 16 of 16; on RFT sand: trained on rigid 0 of 16, trained on rigid + RFT 16 of 16, trained on rigid + MPM 16 of 16; on MPM sand: trained on rigid 0 of 16, trained on rigid + RFT 0 of 16, trained on rigid + MPM 16 of 16.
- Trials without a fall with the tether cut, 1 m/s command, on rigid ground: trained on rigid 16 of 16, trained on rigid + RFT 16 of 16, trained on rigid + MPM 16 of 16; on RFT sand: trained on rigid 0 of 16, trained on rigid + RFT 16 of 16, trained on rigid + MPM 9 of 16; on MPM sand: trained on rigid 0 of 16, trained on rigid + RFT 0 of 16, trained on rigid + MPM 2 of 16.
- Force on the cube at the same cube speed (the 0.5 m/s command, cube at 0.45 to 0.49 m/s): 50 N ± 5 N on rigid ground, 62 N on matched RFT sand (RFT-trained policy; the MPM-trained policy drags the cube at only 0.18 m/s there) and 103 N on MPM sand. This is the like-for-like comparison. At the 1.0 m/s command the cube moves at 0.99 m/s on rigid ground, 0.97 m/s on RFT sand and 0.67 m/s on MPM sand, so the 124 N on MPM sand is not at the speed of the other two and is not compared with them.
- The policies trained on rigid ground and on rigid + RFT do not get as far as steady walking on this MPM sand: 0 of 48 goals, and every trial ends within 1.5 s of the start, with the tether and with it cut. This is not the randomized start. From a start at rest (which still drops the robot about 9.4 cm onto the sand top, as every start on this bed does) the rigid-trained policy falls after 0.84 to 1.42 s and the RFT-trained one after 0.88 to 1.72 s (64 trials each). After standing for 2 s with a zero command, the RFT-trained policy is still up in all 64 trials (tilt at 1.9 s: mean 7°, up to 21°) and falls 0.80 to 1.98 s after the command starts; the rigid-trained policy is already tilting during the stand (mean 13°, up to 22°) and falls 0.00 to 1.28 s after the command (12 of 64 trials in under 0.8 s). So their failure is neither a towing-force result nor a reset result.
- The tether-cut contrast between the three policies is the 0.5 m/s row: on MPM sand the MPM-trained policy stays up in 16 of 16 trials and the other two in 0 of 16. The 1.0 m/s cut row is not a clean walking test for the MPM-trained policy: without the cube it runs at 1.3 m/s with a forward lean of 37 to 38° (with the cube: 0.67 m/s and 20°), close to the 45° tilt limit, and stays up in 15, 9 and 2 of 16 trials on the 0.25, 0.35 and 0.45 m beds (0 of 16 from a start at rest, 3 of 16 after standing 2 s). On sand it was trained only with a cube of 5 to 15 kg.
- Is tow training what removed walking on sand? The walking policies the tow policies were started from, run in this tow evaluation with the tether cut on the 0.45 m bed (16 trials per cell, start as in training; logs `logs/t2/tow_walk_d45`): the RFT walking policy `6t0h2ud8` stays up in 16 of 16 at 0.5 and at 1.0 m/s and walks at 0.46 and 1.01 m/s, feet 12 cm or more above the base, while the tow policy trained from it stays up in 0 of 16. So for the RFT-trained policy, tow training on flat ground did remove walking on this sand. The rigid walking policy `3qymp37c` stays up in only 6 of 16 at 0.5 m/s and 0 of 16 at 1.0 m/s on this bed (falls after 4.2 to 18.9 s and 1.6 to 14.9 s; its feet sink to within 2 cm of the base in 6 and 9 of 16 trials), so for the rigid-trained policy most of the failure is already in the walking policy on deep loose sand and cannot be put on tow training; its earlier 16 of 16 was on the 0.25 m bed. The MPM walking policy `eayrj79r` stays up in 16 of 16 at both speeds.
- Bed depth changes the towing numbers of the MPM-trained policy, which was trained on the 0.25 m bed where its feet reach the base: horizontal pull at the 1.0 m/s command 105 N, 120 N and 124 N on the 0.25, 0.35 and 0.45 m beds, with the cube moving at 0.92, 0.71 and 0.67 m/s. Goal reaching is 16 of 16 at every depth.
- On rigid ground the pull depends on the direction of travel (42 to 56 N over the random headings, cube and ground friction 0.5), which is what a pyramidal friction cone gives; the ± 5 N of the rigid rows is this heading dependence, not trial noise.
- Earlier, smaller gait test (three sands, 0.45 m bed, start above the settled surface; the sweep over 13 sands above replaces it for the pitch-against-tension question). Gait of the MPM-trained policy against the ground (MPM sands on the 0.45 m bed; lowest sole corner 7.4 cm or more above the base in every sand cell). From rigid ground to the three MPM sands the tether tension rises from 60 N to 93 to 136 N and the torso lean from 7 to 9° to 17 to 23°, and the pelvis moves from 14 to 15 cm to 22 to 32 cm ahead of the stance foot. Within the three MPM sands the lean does not follow the tension: it stays between 17 and 23° (the lean penalty starts at 20°) while the tension goes from 93 to 136 N; the pelvis lead does keep growing with the tension (22, 22, 28 cm at 0.5 m/s; 27, 28, 32 cm at 1.0 m/s). On matched RFT sand, outside its training, the policy does not fit the trend: at the 0.5 m/s command it leans 3° and drags the cube at 0.17 m/s. So the lean grows with the required pull between rigid ground and sand, but it saturates near the allowed lean and is not a smooth function of the pull.
- Baselines, on the grounds where they stay up: the RFT-trained policy leans more on RFT sand than on rigid ground (5.8° to 9.6° at 0.5 m/s, 6.2° to 13.8° at 1.0 m/s, tension 59 to 60 N against 73 N), the same direction as the MPM-trained policy. The rigid-trained policy stays up only on rigid ground, so it gives one point per speed.
- Earlier deformation: around the circle the pull falls from 96 N in lap 1 to 88 N in lap 3 (8% lower) while the cube runs 2 cm deeper in its own trench, at a cube speed of 0.40 m/s; a straight tow over fresh sand needs 104 N at 0.47 m/s.

### Not done and remaining differences

- The MPM-trained tow policy was trained on a 0.25 m bed, where its feet come within 2 cm of the rigid base in 16 of 16 towing trials; it is evaluated here on a 0.45 m bed, which it has not seen. It was not retrained on the deeper bed.
- Shared recipe: 1,280 environments, 20,000 tow iterations, 15 kg cube with a mass curriculum from 5 kg on half of the environments (the soft-ground half where there is one), forward lean up to 20° unpenalized, seed 42.
- Differences between the three trainings that remain: (1) mirror loss on with coefficient 0.1 for the rigid and RFT policies, off for the MPM policy (mirror data augmentation on in all three); (2) the PPO runner: compositional for MPM, standard RSL-RL for the others; (3) Newton commit `7adb5be70` with 2 uncommitted entries for the MPM training, `e681453df` clean for the other two; (4) reset position range ±5 m in the RFT task, ±0.5 m in the other two; (5) observation and reward functions follow each task's walking task (base height and foot clearance against the height scanner on MPM, against world height on rigid and RFT); (6) the rigid task's config still holds its walking task's RFT randomization events, which point at a disabled term and do nothing; (7) ground material: RFT default (3,000 kg/m³, 0.3, 0.3) against loose poppy seed on a 0.25 m bed. Sources: the task packages and the bundle logs `logs/t2/osmo/bundle10.log` (MPM) and `bundle11.log` (rigid, RFT).
- All three tow tasks are flat, while the walking policies they start from were trained on rough terrain.
- The RFT-trained tow policy was trained on the RFT task's default material and is tested on RFT sand with the MPM sand's density, internal friction and surface friction; it was not retrained on that material. RFT's generic coefficient table, its contact threshold and its flat, non-deforming surface have no MPM counterpart and are unchanged.
- The cube's rest level is read in the 0.2 to 2 s after it is dropped 2 cm onto the ground, while the tether is still slack; that is 2 to 38 samples per trial. With the tether cut the 0.1 kg cube keeps sinking slowly in the MPM sand, so no static depth of the cube is quoted.
- One training seed per policy. Two evaluation seeds (42, 43) of 8 trials each. One sand material for the comparison, not randomized. One cube. No hardware.
- Provenance: the tow tasks were committed during these runs (IsaacLab `0007b2e67`); before that they were untracked files. The evaluation scripts (`scripts/benchmarks/t2/tow_check.py`, `tow_merge.py`, `tow_report.py`, `tow_gait.py`, `tow_base_clearance.py`) are uncommitted and `tow_check.py` was edited between batches (start options and an early stop were added; the trial loop is otherwise unchanged). `tow_d25`, `tow_d35` and `tow_rft_matched` ran before that edit, the other folders after it. Two code states appear inside single tables: runs before the commit of the tow tasks are on IsaacLab `a60612c79` and Newton `e681453df`, later ones on `0007b2e67` and `48231a989`; the per-folder list below names which. A copy of the scripts and tasks as they stand after the last run, with the diff and status of both repositories, is in `logs/t2/code_snapshot_20261124/`. Each run's JSON holds the commit and tree state of IsaacLab and Newton at the time it ran.
- Videos of the three final checkpoints are not recorded.

## P2 object towing: why the rigid and RFT tow policies fall on sand (T2 session, 2026-11-21)

**Partly superseded, 2026-11-24** (after the independent check, `checks/T2.md`). Step 2 below used earlier tow runs (`g1-tow-rigid-2`, `g1-tow-soft-contact-1`, MPM checkpoint 21,500) on the 0.25 m bed, without foot positions. The tether-cut control and a start-condition control on the three evaluated final checkpoints, on a 0.45 m bed, are in the block "P2 object towing (from the T2 tow policies)" above; use those. Step 1 (the walking policies walk on this sand) was measured on the 0.25 m bed, where T3 later found the walking feet close to the rigid base. Repeated on the 0.45 m bed in the tow evaluation with the tether cut (`logs/t2/tow_walk_d45`, findings of the block above): the RFT walking policy `6t0h2ud8` and the MPM walking policy `eayrj79r` stay up in 16 of 16 at 0.5 and 1.0 m/s; the rigid walking policy `3qymp37c` in 6 of 16 and 0 of 16. So "the walking policy it was started from can walk on the sand" holds for the RFT policy and not for the rigid one on a deep bed. The sentence "cannot walk on the sand at all" below is too strong for what was measured: the tow policies trained on rigid ground and on rigid + RFT fall within 1.5 s of starting to walk, from every start that was tried.

Status: measured in a T2 session, unchecked. Asked for by the lead (steps 1 and 2 of its reply to the towing handoff). Code: IsaacLab `mpm-unification` worktree at `a60612c79`, dirty tree; Newton `e681453df`, clean. GPU: one RTX 5090. Sand depth 0.25 m, voxel 0.04 m (the T3 walking evaluation uses 0.02 m), streaming MPM with the tile cache.

Step 1. Walking policies on flat MPM sand, plain walking task, no object: `scripts/benchmarks/t3/eval_walk.py --angle 0 --terrain ramp --voxel 0.04 --depth 0.25 --num_trials 10 --seconds 13 --travel 5 --seed 41` (`IsaacContrib-Velocity-G1-29dof-MPM-Play`, `physics=solver_cfg_cache`). Start state: position within 0.1 m, heading within 0.1 rad, zero velocity, joint angles scaled 0.9 to 1.1. Raw data `logs/t2/walk_check/`. Velocity error: planar world-frame error after the first second, mean ± std over trials.

| Walking policy | Sand | Command | Goal reached / trials | Falls | Velocity error [m/s] |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| MJWarp-Rough `3qymp37c` | poppy_seed_loose (640 kg/m3, friction 0.73) | 0.5 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.161 ± 0.018 |
| MJWarp-Rough `3qymp37c` | poppy_seed_loose | 1.0 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.185 ± 0.019 |
| MJWarp-Rough `3qymp37c` | sand (1800 kg/m3, friction 0.80, the T3 evaluation sand) | 0.5 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.064 ± 0.008 |
| MJWarp-Rough `3qymp37c` | sand | 1.0 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.098 ± 0.023 |
| RFT-Rough `6t0h2ud8` | poppy_seed_loose | 0.5 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.087 ± 0.008 |
| RFT-Rough `6t0h2ud8` | poppy_seed_loose | 1.0 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.125 ± 0.016 |
| RFT-Rough `6t0h2ud8` | sand | 0.5 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.075 ± 0.004 |
| RFT-Rough `6t0h2ud8` | sand | 1.0 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.127 ± 0.018 |
| MPM-Rough `eayrj79r` | poppy_seed_loose | 0.5 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.053 ± 0.011 |
| MPM-Rough `eayrj79r` | poppy_seed_loose | 1.0 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.079 ± 0.018 |
| MPM-Rough `eayrj79r` | sand | 0.5 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.055 ± 0.007 |
| MPM-Rough `eayrj79r` | sand | 1.0 m/s | 10 / 10 | 0 | 0.085 ± 0.021 |

Step 2. The tow evaluation setup with the rope cut: `scripts/benchmarks/t2/tow_check.py --masses 0 (x16) --seconds 20 --ground sand` (`IsaacContrib-Tow-G1-29dof-MPM-Play`, poppy_seed_loose, the cube lies on the sand untied). "Random start" adds `--randomize`: position within 0.5 m, any heading, linear and angular velocity within 0.5, joint angles scaled 0.5 to 1.5. "Fixed start" is the play configuration's fixed pose. No fall / 16 trials, at 0.5 and 1.0 m/s. Raw data `logs/t2/tow_eval/*-norope*`.

| Policy | Start | No fall at 0.5 m/s | No fall at 1.0 m/s |
|---|---|---|---|
| Walking, MJWarp-Rough `3qymp37c` | random | 16 / 16 | 16 / 16 |
| Walking, MJWarp-Rough `3qymp37c` | fixed | 16 / 16 | 16 / 16 |
| Walking, RFT-Rough `6t0h2ud8` | random | 16 / 16 | 16 / 16 |
| Walking, RFT-Rough `6t0h2ud8` | fixed | 16 / 16 | 16 / 16 |
| Walking, MPM-Rough `eayrj79r` | random | 16 / 16 | 16 / 16 |
| Tow, trained on rigid (`g1-tow-rigid-2`, 39,998) | random | 0 / 16 | 0 / 16 |
| Tow, trained on rigid | fixed | 0 / 16 | 0 / 16 |
| Tow, trained on rigid + RFT (`g1-tow-soft-contact-1`, 39,998) | random | 12 / 16 | 0 / 16 |
| Tow, trained on rigid + RFT | fixed | 16 / 16 | 0 / 16 |
| Tow, trained on rigid + MPM (`g1-tow-mpm-mixed-6`, 21,500) | random | 16 / 16 | 16 / 16 |

What this shows:
- The walking policies walk on this sand in both setups, with either start. The start randomization, the material and the voxel size are not the cause of the falls.
- The rigid tow policy cannot walk on the sand at all, although the walking policy it was started from can. Its 20,000 iterations of tow training on flat rigid ground removed that ability. The RFT tow policy keeps it at 0.5 m/s and loses it at 1.0 m/s.
- The only cell where a baseline walks without the cube and fails with it is the RFT tow policy at 0.5 m/s (16 / 16 without the cube, fixed start; 0 / 16 with the 15 kg cube). That is the one towing failure in this comparison that can be attributed to the cube.
- The walking policies were trained on rough terrain; the rigid and RFT tow tasks are flat only. This difference is not removed by giving all three the same cube-mass schedule and lean penalty.

## P2 object towing: rigid and RFT tow policies retrained with the MPM recipe (T2 session, 2026-11-22)

Owner request: retrain the rigid and RFT tow policies with the same cube-mass curriculum and forward-lean allowance as the MPM tow policy. Submitted 2026-11-22 02:21 PST. All three runs finished at iteration 39,998; W&B `jnskkmr/object_tow`: rigid `aqiwzrv0`, rigid + RFT `9733ux8d`, rigid + MPM `her588wk`. Their evaluation at the final checkpoint is in the block "P2 object towing (from the T2 tow policies)" above. Corrected 2026-11-24 after the independent check (`checks/T2.md`): the code line and the list of differences below.

| | Rigid | Rigid + RFT | Rigid + MPM (reference, already running) |
|---|---|---|---|
| OSMO job | `g1-tow-rigid-3` | `g1-tow-soft-contact-2` | `g1-tow-mpm-mixed-6` (W&B `her588wk`) |
| Task | `IsaacContrib-Tow-G1-29dof-Rigid` | `IsaacContrib-Tow-G1-29dof-SoftContact` | `IsaacContrib-Tow-G1-29dof-MPM-Mixed` |
| Warm start | `3qymp37c` | `6t0h2ud8` | `eayrj79r` |
| Environments | 4 GPUs x 320 = 1,280 | 4 GPUs x 320 = 1,280 | 40 GPUs x 32 = 1,280 |
| Iterations | 20,000 | 20,000 | 20,000 |
| Ground | flat rigid | flat, half rigid, half RFT | flat, half rigid, half MPM sand |
| Cube mass | half of the environments 5 to 15 kg by curriculum, half fixed 15 kg | RFT half 5 to 15 kg by curriculum, rigid half fixed 15 kg | sand half 5 to 15 kg by curriculum, rigid half fixed 15 kg |
| Orientation penalty | forward lean up to 20 degrees free | same | same |

Code, from the bundle logs `logs/t2/osmo/bundle11.log` (rigid, RFT) and `bundle10.log` (MPM): rigid and RFT runs IsaacLab `a60612c79` with 67 uncommitted entries, Newton `e681453df`, clean; MPM run IsaacLab `a60612c79` with 66 uncommitted entries, Newton `7adb5be70` with 2 uncommitted entries (two commits before `e681453df`: the memo fix in the coupled attribute projection and the tile cache skipping worlds without particles came after). Warp `3d0f4e5b7`, clean, for all. The curriculum term and the lean reward are the MPM tow task's functions (`g1_29dof_mpm_tow/mdp`), used by all three tasks. Seed 42 for all three (the config default; no `--seed` was passed).

Differences that remain:
- Terrain: all three tow tasks are flat. The walking policies they start from were trained on rough terrain. The earlier flat rigid tow policy lost its ability to walk on MPM sand (section above); this retrain does not address that.
- PPO runner: the MPM task uses the compositional runner; the rigid and RFT tasks the standard RSL-RL runner with their walking tasks' settings.
- Observations and rewards follow each task's walking task (for example the base height is measured against the height scanner on MPM and against world height on rigid and RFT); only the orientation penalty and the cube-mass schedule were made the same.
- RFT sand is one fixed default material (critical density 3,000 kg/m3, internal friction 0.3, surface friction 0.3); MPM sand is loose poppy seed (640 kg/m3, 0.73) on a 0.25 m bed. Neither is randomized. The rigid task's config still holds the RFT randomization events of its walking task, but they point at a disabled RFT term and do nothing.
- Mirror loss: on with coefficient 0.1 for the rigid and RFT runs, off for the MPM run. Mirror data augmentation is on in all three. Read from `g1_29dof_{rigid,soft_contact}_tow/agents/rsl_rl_ppo_cfg.py:51-53` and `g1_29dof_mpm_tow/agents/compositional_ppo_cfg.py:59`.
- Reset position range: within 5 m for the RFT task, within 0.5 m for the rigid and MPM tasks (`env_cfg/event_cfg.py` of each package).
- Newton commit: `7adb5be70` with uncommitted changes for the MPM training, `e681453df` for the other two and for every evaluation.
- One seed each (42).
<!-- T2-TOW-RETRAIN:end -->
